Because the second that alarm goes off and I open my eyes, boom, I'm awake, and that heavy gray feeling I was telling you about is just instantly sitting right there on my chest again. But as far as actually falling asleep or staying asleep? No, I haven't had any trouble with that at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re not worth much or that you’re failing at things you used to handle easily?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say that at all. I don't feel worthless, and I definitely don't feel like I'm failing at things. \n\nI mean, I know I'm good at what I do. I've been in the landscaping business for, what, almost thirty years now? I started right out of high school working for this guy, old man Peterson, just hauling bags of mulch and pushing a basic rotary mower around, and I learned the trade from the ground up. I can walk onto a property and look at a patchy lawn and tell you exactly what's wrong with it—whether it's a grub infestation, or a nitrogen deficiency, or if the irrigation heads are just spaced wrong. \n\nLike, just this past Thursday, we were on a commercial site over by the interstate, and the property manager was trying to tell me the retaining wall we put in two seasons ago was failing. I went right out there, checked the grade, and showed him exactly how the water runoff from the new asphalt parking lot they just poured next door was undercutting the base. I had to explain the whole hydrostatic pressure thing to him. It wasn't our work failing at all, it was the civil engineering next door. So I know my worth on the job, you know? I can still handle all the technical stuff, the crew management, the physical labor. I'm not dropping the ball there.\n\nAnd at home, it's the same thing. I'm still paying the bills on time, still keeping a roof over our heads. I don't feel like a failure as a husband or a father. I actually just fixed the garbage disposal under the kitchen sink yesterday evening. My wife thought we were going to have to call a plumber and spend two hundred bucks, but I knew it was just a jammed impeller. Took me maybe ten minutes with a quarter-inch hex wrench to get it spinning freely again. \n\nSo my confidence... or my self-esteem, or whatever you want to call it... that's all completely intact. I know I'm a hard worker. I know I'm a reliable guy. It's just... it all comes back to what I was saying before. It's that heavy, gray feeling *while* I'm doing all these things that I know I'm perfectly capable of doing. I don't look in the mirror and think I'm a piece of garbage or a failure, not at all. I just look in the mirror and feel that constant weight. But no, as far as feeling worthless? I haven't had any thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight lately—like eating less than usual or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. My appetite hasn't changed a bit, and my weight is pretty much exactly where it's always been. I mean, you gotta figure, with the kind of work I do, you burn a ton of calories just being out in the sun all day hauling brush and wrestling with the heavy equipment. If I didn't eat, I'd be passing out behind the wheel of the zero-turn mower, and you definitely don't want that happening when you're running blades at that speed over uneven terrain. So, no, I'm eating just as much as I always have. \n\nLike, every morning, my wife packs my Igloo cooler—the little red and white one, I've had it forever, the handle is actually held together with some duct tape right now—and she always puts in a good, solid lunch. Usually a couple of sandwiches, like ham and Swiss on rye with a little mustard, maybe a bag of those jalapeño kettle chips I like, an apple, and a couple of Gatorades. And when noon rolls around, like I was telling you before about sitting on the tailgate with the crew, I eat every last bite of it. \n\nAnd then for dinner, my wife, she's a real traditional cook, you know? She makes sure there's a hot meal on the table every night at 6:30 sharp. Meatloaf, mashed potatoes, green beans... or maybe she'll do a big baked ziti, which is my favorite. And I always clean my plate. I might even go back for seconds if it's the ziti. \n\nSo my weight hasn't budged at all. I've been hovering right around 195, maybe 200 pounds depending on the season, for the last ten or fifteen years. My work pants—I wear those heavy-duty Carhartt canvas pants because the briars and thorns just tear right through regular denim when we're clearing out overgrown lots—they still fit exactly the same. My belt is on the exact same notch it was on five years ago. \n\nI guess the only thing I'd say about the food, and this just goes right back to that heavy, gray feeling I keep talking about, is that I just don't really care about it. Like, I'm eating it, and I'm chewing it, and I know it's good food because my wife is a great cook, but I'm just... I'm just fueling the machine, you know? It's just calories to keep my arms moving and my legs walking so I can get the job done. I don't really get any joy out of a good steak or a piece of pie anymore. But as far as actually losing my appetite or dropping pounds without trying? No, nothing like that at all. I'm taking in plenty of food.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you've been moving more slowly than usual or that you can't sit still, maybe feeling restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, my pace is pretty much exactly what it's always been. You gotta understand, in the landscaping business, time is literally money. We bid these jobs based on man-hours, so if I start moving in slow motion out there, the whole schedule gets thrown off, and we end up losing daylight. \n\nLike just on Tuesday, we had a massive property cleanup over in that new subdivision on the west side of town—we were clearing out all the builder's debris, rough-grading the topsoil, and laying down maybe four or five pallets of Bermuda sod. If I was moving slower than usual, my foreman, Dave—he's this younger guy, maybe thirty-two, always checking his smartwatch and stressing about the route sheet—he would've been on my case in a heartbeat. But I was out there swinging the pickaxe, tossing the sod rolls, running the walk-behind aerator at the exact same clip I always do. My body just knows the rhythm, you know? I just put my head down, put my work gloves on, and go. I don't drag my feet.\n\nAnd as far as being restless or fidgety, or not being able to sit still? No, definitely not that either. I don't tap my foot, I don't wring my hands or pace around the house. Honestly, like I was telling you before about that brown leather recliner of mine... once I sit down in that thing after a long day in the heat, I am absolutely planted. I don't bounce my leg or get up and down during the commercial breaks. My wife is actually the one who can't sit still—she's always popping up to wipe down the kitchen counters again or fold a load of towels while we're trying to watch a show, and I'm just sitting there, completely still, just staring at the screen. \n\nSo my physical movements, my speed, all of that is perfectly normal. It's just... internally, I guess. The engine is running at the exact same RPM it always has, doing the exact same physical work, but the guy driving the machine just feels... blank. Just carrying that same heavy weight I mentioned. But physically? No, I'm not moving slow or bouncing off the walls or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like life isn’t worth living or thought about harming yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, no... no, I haven't. I mean, I know what you're asking, and I understand why you'd ask it, especially with me talking about this constant, heavy, gray feeling all the time. But no, I don't sit around thinking that life isn't worth living, and I definitely haven't had any thoughts about harming myself. Nothing like that. \n\nI guess the best way I can explain it is... I'm still showing up for life, you know? I still get up at 4:30 every morning when the alarm goes off. It's this old digital clock radio I've had since maybe 1998, with the red numbers, and it always goes off right at 4:30. I get up, I make the coffee—just regular Folgers, nothing fancy, run it through the drip machine—and I sit there at the kitchen table in the dark before my wife wakes up, just drinking my coffee and going over the route sheet for the day in my head. I'm planning out which crew is taking the big trailer with the two zero-turns, and who's taking the smaller setup for the residential stuff over in the historic district where the driveways are too narrow for the big rigs. I'm still engaged in all of that. \n\nIf I thought life wasn't worth living, I wouldn't care about any of that, right? I wouldn't care if the blades on the mowers were sharpened or if the two-cycle oil was mixed right for the trimmers. But I do care. I mean, I have responsibilities. I have a wife who relies on me, I have a mortgage on a three-bedroom ranch house that we bought fifteen years ago, and I have guys on my crew who need me to keep the business running so they can feed their own families. Like my foreman, Dave, he just had his second kid, a little girl. If I just gave up, what happens to Dave? What happens to his family? \n\nSo, no, I don't want to die or hurt myself. I'm not looking for an exit. It's just... it's just this enduring slog. It's like I'm walking through waist-deep mud every single day. I'm still walking, I'm still putting one foot in front of the other, and I'm still getting to where I need to go. It just takes so much effort to drag myself through it, and there's no joy in the journey anymore. I'm just doing what needs to be done because I'm a responsible guy and that's what you do. You work hard, you pay your bills, you take care of your people. But as far as actually wanting to end it all? No, absolutely not. I've never had a thought like that cross my mind.\n\nDoctor: Even though you still do the things you enjoy, like fishing or helping your daughter with her project, do you find that you don’t feel any pleasure or satisfaction while doing them?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I still get a kick out of the things I really like doing. It's not like I'm completely numb or anything. Like with the fishing... if I take my old fourteen-foot aluminum jon boat out to the reservoir—usually I try to get out there by 5:30 in the morning on a Sunday, right when the mist is still coming off the water, you know? I'll take my favorite rod, it's just a medium-heavy action with a baitcaster reel, and I'll tie on a chartreuse spinnerbait. And I'll tell you, when I'm casting out near the lily pads and I feel that sudden jerk on the line, that heavy thump when a good-sized largemouth bass hits the lure... yeah, I definitely still feel that rush. I get a lot of satisfaction out of reeling it in, netting it, just admiring the fish before I toss it back. I smile. I enjoy it. I really do. \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing with my daughter. She's twenty-two now, just rented this little duplex over on the east side, and she wanted to put in some raised garden beds for tomatoes and peppers. Well, being in landscaping, that's right up my alley, so I went over there last weekend to help her. We drove my truck down to the lumber yard and picked out some nice, thick cedar planks—you always want to use cedar or redwood for vegetables, never the pressure-treated stuff because the chemicals can leach into the soil, and I explained all that to her. We spent the whole afternoon measuring, cutting the boards, driving in these three-inch star-bit deck screws so they won't strip out... and when we finally got them all boxed up and perfectly leveled out in her yard, and I saw how proud she was? I felt great. I felt real pleasure in that. We sat on the tailgate and drank a couple of Cokes and just admired the work, and I felt like a good dad. \n\nSo, no, when I'm actually doing those things, the fishing, the projects with her, I'm definitely feeling the satisfaction. It's just that... well, the fishing trip ends, or the garden beds are built, and I have to go back to the regular day-to-day, and that's when that heavy, gray slog I was telling you about just settles right back over my shoulders. But the actual activities themselves? No, I haven't lost the joy in those. I still feel it just fine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, I wouldn't say that either. It's not just the fishing or the big projects that I like. I still enjoy plenty of regular, everyday things, too. 

I mean, take Friday nights, for example. My wife and I, we have this little tradition where I'll stop by Miller's Butcher Shop on the way home from the yard. It's this family-owned place off Route 9—they've been there since I was a kid, actually, I went to high school with the owner's younger brother, Mike. Anyway, I'll go in and pick up two nice, thick ribeyes. Bone-in, usually, because the flavor is just better. I take them home, rub them down with just some coarse kosher salt, cracked black pepper, and maybe a little garlic powder, and then I throw them on my Weber kettle grill. I've always preferred charcoal over gas. I use those Kingsford briquettes, and I always get them going in a metal chimney starter so you don't get that nasty lighter fluid taste on the meat. When I'm standing out there on the back patio, smelling that smoke, holding a cold beer, and then we finally sit down at the table and that steak is cooked a perfect medium-rare... I enjoy the heck out of that. That gives me a lot of pleasure. A really good meal still tastes just as good as it always did. 

And it's the same thing with other stuff, too. Like if I'm watching a football game on a Sunday afternoon. I've been a die-hard fan of my team for thirty years, through all the terrible seasons and the good ones. If they pull off a big win in the fourth quarter, or the quarterback throws a perfect fifty-yard bomb down the sideline, I'm out of my seat, cheering, high-fiving my wife, just feeling really good about it. Or even just listening to classic rock on the radio while I'm driving the work truck between job sites—if a good Bob Seger song comes on, I'll turn it up and sing along, and I genuinely enjoy it. 

So it's not like everything else is just dead to me, or like everything tastes like cardboard. I still like a good joke, I still like a good meal, I still like my sports. It's just... like I was trying to explain before, it's the background noise. It's what happens when the steak is eaten, or the game goes off, or the song ends, and I'm just left sitting there with my own thoughts. That's when I feel that heavy, gray slog I was talking about. But the actual things in life? The things that are supposed to be fun or taste good? No, I still get plenty of enjoyment out of them. I'm not walking around completely numb to the good stuff. I just wanted to make sure I'm explaining that right, because I know it sounds a little contradictory. You'd think if a guy feels like he's dragging a hundred-pound weight around all day, he wouldn't care about a ribeye steak or a football game, but I really do. I still enjoy all that stuff just fine.

I just can't shake that low feeling, no matter what time it is on the clock.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping, like difficulty falling asleep, waking up during the night, or waking up earlier than you'd like and not being able to go back to sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. Sleep is about the only thing that actually comes easy these days, to be honest. I mean, you gotta figure, with the kind of physical labor I do out there in the yards all day, by the time I get home, my body is just completely beat. I'll just be sitting there watching a baseball game or whatever's on the History Channel, and next thing I know, my wife is nudging my shoulder around 9:30 or 10 telling me to get up and go sleep in the actual bed. \n\nAnd once I drag myself down the hall and my head hits that pillow in the bedroom, I am just gone. It's the waking up. But as far as actually losing my appetite or dropping pounds without trying? No, nothing like that at all. I'm not looking for an exit. It's just... it's just this enduring slog. It's like I'm walking through waist-deep mud every single day. I'm still walking, I'm still putting one foot in front of the other, and I'm still getting to where I need to go. It just takes so much effort to drag myself through it, and there's no joy in the journey anymore. I'm just doing what needs to be done because I'm a responsible guy and that's what you do. You work hard, you pay your bills, you take care of your people. But as far as actually wanting to end it all? No, absolutely not. I've never had a thought like that cross my mind.\n\nDoctor: Even though you still do the things you enjoy, like fishing or helping your daughter with her project, do you find that you don’t feel any pleasure or satisfaction while doing them?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say that. When I'm standing out there on the back patio, smelling that smoke, holding a cold beer, and then we finally sit down at the table and that steak is cooked a perfect medium-rare... It’s right there on my chest before I even open my eyes. My alarm goes off at 5 AM—I do landscaping and groundskeeping, so we start real early to beat the heat—and usually, you know, a guy just gets up, puts his boots on, and gets to it. But lately, it’s just... it’s this massive effort. \n\nAnd that weight, it just doesn't lift. I get out there with the crew, and we’re mowing or laying mulch or whatever the contract is for the day, and the guys are all joking around, talking about the game or their trucks, and I’m just... I’m there, physically, but I can’t shake this low, hollow feeling. It’s like I’m completely empty inside. \n\nIt doesn't even stop when I clock out. Even when I get home and I'm around my kids... and you’re supposed to be happy around your kids, right? You’re supposed to ask them about school and actually feel something. But I sit down at the dinner table and I feel like I’m just going through the motions. I nod, I say the right things, I tell them to do their homework, but I’m not really there. It’s just this constant, dragging grayness, no matter where I am or what I’m doing. It doesn't matter if it's a beautiful sunny day out on a property or if I'm sitting in my own living room, I just can't seem to snap out of it, and I don't know how to just be a normal guy anymore because... well, it's just always there, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost interest in things you used to enjoy, like spending time with your kids or any hobbies?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know if I'd say I've lost interest. I haven't really noticed anything like that, to be honest. I mean, I still want to do the things I've always done. Like with my hobbies, I'm a big fisherman, mostly going after largemouth bass out on the reservoir, and I still spend hours out in the garage organizing my tackle box. I've got this whole system, you see, with the crankbaits and topwater lures in the top trays and the soft plastics sorted by color down below in these little ziplock bags so they don't dry out. I spent a good three hours out there just this past Sunday making sure all the hooks were sharp, using this little diamond file I picked up at the hardware store a few weeks back. If I'd lost interest, I wouldn't be out there doing all that tedious work, right? I still care about it. \n\nAnd with the kids, my youngest daughter is doing this science fair project on soil erosion—which is right up my alley with the landscaping and all—and I’m fully invested in helping her figure out the right mix of sand and clay to use for her display. We went down to the garden center, picked out three different types of topsoil, and I was explaining to her how the drainage works and how the roots hold the earth together. I really want her to win that blue ribbon, I really do. \n\nSo the interest is definitely still there, 100 percent. It's not that I don't care or don't want to do these things. I'm still doing all of it, still planning my weekends around it, still reading up on new fishing gear when I get the catalogs in the mail. It's just... like I was saying before, when I'm actually in the middle of doing it, that heavy gray feeling is just sitting right on top of me. But the interest itself? No, I haven't noticed that going away at all. I still want to do it all, I just... I don't know, it just feels heavy while I'm doing it. But no, I haven't lost interest.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your mood is depressed or that you feel down most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. Like I was trying to say right at the beginning, it's... it's pretty much all the time. Every single day. I mean, my wife, she'll ask me sometimes, \"Are you just having a bad Tuesday?\" and I have to tell her, it's not just Tuesday. It's every day. It's exactly like I was saying about that gray weight. It's not just a passing thing where I get a little bummed out because it started raining and we had to tarp up the mowers and lose an hour of work. \n\nIt's... well, my alarm goes off at 4:30, right? And before my feet even hit the floorboards, before I even smell the coffee she set up the night before, I just feel down. Just this deep, heavy, down feeling. And it stays with me the entire day. I get in my truck—it's an older F-150, the heater takes forever to kick in—and I drive to the depot, and that whole twenty-minute drive in the dark, it's just this hollow emptiness sitting right in my chest. \n\nThen we're out there, like I said, with the crew. And maybe it's noon, the sun's beating down, guys are sitting on the tailgate eating their sandwiches, and I'm just chewing my food, totally going through the motions. It doesn't lift at 2 PM, it doesn't lift at 5 PM when we finally pack up the weed whackers and clock out. And even when I get home, like I mentioned with the kids at the dinner table, it's just... it's constant. So, yeah, \"most of the day, nearly every day\" is hitting the nail right on the head. It's seven days a week, doc. Monday through Sunday, weekends and holidays included. I just can't shake that low feeling, no matter what time it is on the clock.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping, like difficulty falling asleep, waking up during the night, or waking up earlier than you'd like and not being able to go back to sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. Sleep is about the only thing that actually comes easy these days, to be honest. I mean, you gotta figure, with the kind of physical labor I do out there in the yards all day, by the time I get home, my body is just completely beat. I usually take off my work boots out on the back porch so I don't track mud and grass clippings into the house—my wife hates that, especially if she just ran the Swiffer over the kitchen linoleum—and then I go straight in and take a hot shower just to wash the day's dirt off my neck. \n\nBy the time we have dinner and I finally sit down in my recliner in the living room... it's this old brown leather one I've had for maybe fifteen years, most comfortable chair in the world, even if the stuffing is coming out of the left armrest... I'm usually out cold before the eight o'clock shows are even half over. I'll just be sitting there watching a baseball game or whatever's on the History Channel, and next thing I know, my wife is nudging my shoulder around 9:30 or 10 telling me to get up and go sleep in the actual bed. \n\nAnd once I drag myself down the hall and my head hits that pillow in the bedroom, I am just gone. I don't toss and turn, I don't wake up at 2 AM staring at the ceiling or anything like that. I sleep like a log straight through the night. And I definitely don't wake up early on my own, either. I sleep right up until that buzzer goes off at 4:30 AM. \n\nSo the sleeping part itself is fine. I'm getting plenty of rest, I guess. It's just... the problem isn't the sleeping, you know? It's the waking up. Because the second that alarm goes off and I open my eyes, boom, I'm awake, and that heavy gray feeling I was telling you about is just instantly sitting right there on my chest again. But as far as actually falling asleep or staying asleep? No, I haven't had any trouble with that at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you’re not worth much or that you’re failing at things you used to handle easily?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say that at all. I don't feel worthless, and I definitely don't feel like I'm failing at things. \n\nI mean, I know I'm good at what I do. I've been in the landscaping business for, what, almost thirty years now? I started right out of high school working for this guy, old man Peterson, just hauling bags of mulch and pushing a basic rotary mower around, and I learned the trade from the ground up. I can walk onto a property and look at a patchy lawn and tell you exactly what's wrong with it—whether it's a grub infestation, or a nitrogen deficiency, or if the irrigation heads are just spaced wrong. \n\nLike, just this past Thursday, we were on a commercial site over by the interstate, and the property manager was trying to tell me the retaining wall we put in two seasons ago was failing. I went right out there, checked the grade, and showed him exactly how the water runoff from the new asphalt parking lot they just poured next door was undercutting the base. I had to explain the whole hydrostatic pressure thing to him. It wasn't our work failing at all, it was the civil engineering next door. So I know my worth on the job, you know? I can still handle all the technical stuff, the crew management, the physical labor. I'm not dropping the ball there.\n\nAnd at home, it's the same thing. I'm still paying the bills on time, still keeping a roof over our heads. I don't feel like a failure as a husband or a father. I actually just fixed the garbage disposal under the kitchen sink yesterday evening. My wife thought we were going to have to call a plumber and spend two hundred bucks, but I knew it was just a jammed impeller. Took me maybe ten minutes with a quarter-inch hex wrench to get it spinning freely again. \n\nSo my confidence... or my self-esteem, or whatever you want to call it... that's all completely intact. I know I'm a hard worker. I know I'm a reliable guy. It's just... it all comes back to what I was saying before. It's that heavy, gray feeling *while* I'm doing all these things that I know I'm perfectly capable of doing. I don't look in the mirror and think I'm a piece of garbage or a failure, not at all. I just look in the mirror and feel that constant weight. But no, as far as feeling worthless? I haven't had any thoughts like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight lately—like eating less than usual or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. My appetite hasn't changed a bit, and my weight is pretty much exactly where it's always been. I mean, you gotta figure, with the kind of work I do, you burn a ton of calories just being out in the sun all day hauling brush and wrestling with the heavy equipment. If I didn't eat, I'd be passing out behind the wheel of the zero-turn mower, and you definitely don't want that happening when you're running blades at that speed over uneven terrain. So, no, I'm eating just as much as I always have. \n\nLike, every morning, my wife packs my Igloo cooler—the little red and white one, I've had it forever, the handle is actually held together with some duct tape right now—and she always puts in a good, solid lunch. Usually a couple of sandwiches, like ham and Swiss on rye with a little mustard, maybe a bag of those jalapeño kettle chips I like, an apple, and a couple of Gatorades. And when noon rolls around, like I was telling you before about sitting on the tailgate with the crew, I eat every last bite of it. \n\nAnd then for dinner, my wife, she's a real traditional cook, you know? She makes sure there's a hot meal on the table every night at 6:30 sharp. Meatloaf, mashed potatoes, green beans... or maybe she'll do a big baked ziti, which is my favorite. And I always clean my plate. I might even go back for seconds if it's the ziti. \n\nSo my weight hasn't budged at all. I've been hovering right around 195, maybe 200 pounds depending on the season, for the last ten or fifteen years. My work pants—I wear those heavy-duty Carhartt canvas pants because the briars and thorns just tear right through regular denim when we're clearing out overgrown lots—they still fit exactly the same. My belt is on the exact same notch it was on five years ago. \n\nI guess the only thing I'd say about the food, and this just goes right back to that heavy, gray feeling I keep talking about, is that I just don't really care about it. Like, I'm eating it, and I'm chewing it, and I know it's good food because my wife is a great cook, but I'm just... I'm just fueling the machine, you know? It's just calories to keep my arms moving and my legs walking so I can get the job done. I don't really get any joy out of a good steak or a piece of pie anymore. But as far as actually losing my appetite or dropping pounds without trying? No, nothing like that at all. I'm taking in plenty of food.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you've been moving more slowly than usual or that you can't sit still, maybe feeling restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, my pace is pretty much exactly what it's always been. You gotta understand, in the landscaping business, time is literally money. We bid these jobs based on man-hours, so if I start moving in slow motion out there, the whole schedule gets thrown off, and we end up losing daylight. \n\nLike just on Tuesday, we had a massive property cleanup over in that new subdivision on the west side of town—we were clearing out all the builder's debris, rough-grading the topsoil, and laying down maybe four or five pallets of Bermuda sod. If I was moving slower than usual, my foreman, Dave—he's this younger guy, maybe thirty-two, always checking his smartwatch and stressing about the route sheet—he would've been on my case in a heartbeat. But I was out there swinging the pickaxe, tossing the sod rolls, running the walk-behind aerator at the exact same clip I always do. My body just knows the rhythm, you know? I just put my head down, put my work gloves on, and go. I don't drag my feet.\n\nAnd as far as being restless or fidgety, or not being able to sit still? No, definitely not that either. I don't tap my foot, I don't wring my hands or pace around the house. Honestly, like I was telling you before about that brown leather recliner of mine... once I sit down in that thing after a long day in the heat, I am absolutely planted. I don't bounce my leg or get up and down during the commercial breaks. My wife is actually the one who can't sit still—she's always popping up to wipe down the kitchen counters again or fold a load of towels while we're trying to watch a show, and I'm just sitting there, completely still, just staring at the screen. \n\nSo my physical movements, my speed, all of that is perfectly normal. It's just... internally, I guess. The engine is running at the exact same RPM it always has, doing the exact same physical work, but the guy driving the machine just feels... blank. Just carrying that same heavy weight I mentioned. But physically? No, I'm not moving slow or bouncing off the walls or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like life isn’t worth living or thought about harming yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, no... no, I haven't. I mean, I know what you're asking, and I understand why you'd ask it, especially with me talking about this constant, heavy, gray feeling all the time. But no, I don't sit around thinking that life isn't worth living, and I definitely haven't had any thoughts about harming myself. Nothing like that. \n\nI guess the best way I can explain it is... I'm still showing up for life, you know? I still get up at 4:30 every morning when the alarm goes off. It's this old digital clock radio I've had since maybe 1998, with the red numbers, and it always goes off right at 4:30. I get up, I make the coffee—just regular Folgers, nothing fancy, run it through the drip machine—and I sit there at the kitchen table in the dark before my wife wakes up, just drinking my coffee and going over the route sheet for the day in my head. I'm planning out which crew is taking the big trailer with the two zero-turns, and who's taking the smaller setup for the residential stuff over in the historic district where the driveways are too narrow for the big rigs. I'm still engaged in all of that. \n\nIf I thought life wasn't worth living, I wouldn't care about any of that, right? I wouldn't care if the blades on the mowers were sharpened or if the two-cycle oil was mixed right for the trimmers. But I do care. I mean, I have responsibilities. I have a wife who relies on me, I have a mortgage on a three-bedroom ranch house that we bought fifteen years ago, and I have guys on my crew who need me to keep the business running so they can feed their own families. Like my foreman, Dave, he just had his second kid, a little girl. If I just gave up, what happens to Dave? What happens to his family? \n\nSo, no, I don't want to die or hurt myself. I'm not looking for an exit. It's just... it's just this enduring slog. It's like I'm walking through waist-deep mud every single day. I'm still walking, I'm still putting one foot in front of the other, and I'm still getting to where I need to go. It just takes so much effort to drag myself through it, and there's no joy in the journey anymore. I'm just doing what needs to be done because I'm a responsible guy and that's what you do. You work hard, you pay your bills, you take care of your people. But as far as actually wanting to end it all? No, absolutely not. I've never had a thought like that cross my mind.\n\nDoctor: Even though you still do the things you enjoy, like fishing or helping your daughter with her project, do you find that you don’t feel any pleasure or satisfaction while doing them?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I still get a kick out of the things I really like doing. It's not like I'm completely numb or anything. Like with the fishing... if I take my old fourteen-foot aluminum jon boat out to the reservoir—usually I try to get out there by 5:30 in the morning on a Sunday, right when the mist is still coming off the water, you know? I'll take my favorite rod, it's just a medium-heavy action with a baitcaster reel, and I'll tie on a chartreuse spinnerbait. And I'll tell you, when I'm casting out near the lily pads and I feel that sudden jerk on the line, that heavy thump when a good-sized largemouth bass hits the lure... yeah, I definitely still feel that rush. I get a lot of satisfaction out of reeling it in, netting it, just admiring the fish before I toss it back. I smile. I enjoy it. I really do. \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing with my daughter. She's twenty-two now, just rented this little duplex over on the east side, and she wanted to put in some raised garden beds for tomatoes and peppers. Well, being in landscaping, that's right up my alley, so I went over there last weekend to help her. We drove my truck down to the lumber yard and picked out some nice, thick cedar planks—you always want to use cedar or redwood for vegetables, never the pressure-treated stuff because the chemicals can leach into the soil, and I explained all that to her. We spent the whole afternoon measuring, cutting the boards, driving in these three-inch star-bit deck screws so they won't strip out... and when we finally got them all boxed up and perfectly leveled out in her yard, and I saw how proud she was? I felt great. I felt real pleasure in that. We sat on the tailgate and drank a couple of Cokes and just admired the work, and I felt like a good dad. \n\nSo, no, when I'm actually doing those things, the fishing, the projects with her, I'm definitely feeling the satisfaction. It's just that... well, the fishing trip ends, or the garden beds are built, and I have to go back to the regular day-to-day, and that's when that heavy, gray slog I was telling you about just settles right back over my shoulders. But the actual activities themselves? No, I haven't lost the joy in those. I still feel it just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that, outside of those specific activities like fishing or working with your daughter, most things in life don’t give you much pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say that either. It's not just the fishing or the big projects that I like. I still enjoy plenty of regular, everyday things, too. \n\nI mean, take Friday nights, for example. My wife and I, we have this little tradition where I'll stop by Miller's Butcher Shop on the way home from the yard. It's this family-owned place off Route 9—they've been there since I was a kid, actually, I went to high school with the owner's younger brother, Mike. Anyway, I'll go in and pick up two nice, thick ribeyes. Bone-in, usually, because the flavor is just better. I take them home, rub them down with just some coarse kosher salt, cracked black pepper, and maybe a little garlic powder, and then I throw them on my Weber kettle grill. I've always preferred charcoal over gas. I use those Kingsford briquettes, and I always get them going in a metal chimney starter so you don't get that nasty lighter fluid taste on the meat. When I'm standing out there on the back patio, smelling that smoke, holding a cold beer, and then we finally sit down at the table and that steak is cooked a perfect medium-rare... I enjoy the heck out of that. That gives me a lot of pleasure. A really good meal still tastes just as good as it always did. \n\nAnd it's the same thing with other stuff, too. Like if I'm watching a football game on a Sunday afternoon. I've been a die-hard fan of my team for thirty years, through all the terrible seasons and the good ones. If they pull off a big win in the fourth quarter, or the quarterback throws a perfect fifty-yard bomb down the sideline, I'm out of my seat, cheering, high-fiving my wife, just feeling really good about it. Or even just listening to classic rock on the radio while I'm driving the work truck between job sites—if a good Bob Seger song comes on, I'll turn it up and sing along, and I genuinely enjoy it. \n\nSo it's not like everything else is just dead to me, or like everything tastes like cardboard. I still like a good joke, I still like a good meal, I still like my sports. It's just... like I was trying to explain before, it's the background noise. It's what happens when the steak is eaten, or the game goes off, or the song ends, and I'm just left sitting there with my own thoughts. That's when I feel that heavy, gray slog I was talking about. But the actual things in life? The things that are supposed to be fun or taste good? No, I still get plenty of enjoyment out of them. I'm not walking around completely numb to the good stuff. I just wanted to make sure I'm explaining that right, because I know it sounds a little contradictory. You'd think if a guy feels like he's dragging a hundred-pound weight around all day, he wouldn't care about a ribeye steak or a football game, but I really do. I still enjoy all that stuff just fine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Well, I've been trying to think if there was ever a time like that, but honestly... no, I really haven't noticed anything like that. Not even for a day. I mean, it’s a fair question, but if I’m eating that ribeye, I’m enjoying it. Every single time. 

Take last November, for instance. Right around Thanksgiving, we had this massive, unexpected cold snap. The ground froze solid way earlier than usual, which is an absolute nightmare in my line of work because we were still trying to finish up a big hardscaping job over at that new subdivision on Elm Street. Have you seen those new houses? They're huge, but the lots are tiny. Anyway, laying patio pavers when the ground is freezing up is just... well, it's a massive headache. You have to bring in these expensive ground heaters, the polymeric sand doesn't settle right between the joints, the guys on the crew are miserable... I was stressed out of my mind, working fourteen-hour days, coming home just completely wiped out, bone-tired, and that heavy, gray slog feeling I told you about was just crushing me worse than ever. 

But even then, even on the absolute worst day of that week—it was a Thursday, I remember specifically because my work truck's heater core decided to blow out on the drive home, so I was freezing my tail off in the cab—when I finally walked through the front door and my wife had made a big pot of her homemade chili... man, that chili tasted amazing. She uses the ground chuck from Miller's, the same place I get the steaks, and she puts in these dark red kidney beans and just the right amount of cumin and chili powder. I sat down at the kitchen table, ate two massive bowls of it, put on the Thursday night football game, and for those couple of hours, I was completely locked in. I loved every single bite, and I was yelling at the TV when the refs blew a totally obvious holding call in the third quarter. 

So, even on my absolute worst days, when that heavy feeling is just pushing me down into the dirt, the good stuff still feels good. The food still tastes great, the games are still exciting, the fishing is still fun. It never really dips or goes away, not even for a day or two. It's just that the minute the game is over and the TV goes off, or the bowl is empty, I'm right back in the mud. But the enjoyment itself? No, I've never lost that.

Honestly, like I was telling you before about that brown leather recliner of mine... once I sit down in that thing after a long day in the heat, I am absolutely planted. I don't bounce my leg or get up and down during the commercial breaks. My wife is actually the one who can't sit still—she's always popping up to wipe down the kitchen counters again or fold a load of towels while we're trying to watch a show, and I'm just sitting there, completely still, just staring at the screen. \n\nSo my physical movements, my speed, all of that is perfectly normal. It's just... internally, I guess. The engine is running at the exact same RPM it always has, doing the exact same physical work, but the guy driving the machine just feels... blank. Just carrying that same heavy weight I mentioned. But physically? No, I'm not moving slow or bouncing off the walls or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like life isn’t worth living or thought about harming yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, no... no, I haven't. I mean, I know what you're asking, and I understand why you'd ask it, especially with me talking about this constant, heavy, gray feeling all the time. But no, I don't sit around thinking that life isn't worth living, and I definitely haven't had any thoughts about harming myself. Nothing like that. \n\nI guess the best way I can explain it is... I'm still showing up for life, you know? I still get up at 4:30 every morning when the alarm goes off. It's this old digital clock radio I've had since maybe 1998, with the red numbers, and it always goes off right at 4:30. I get up, I make the coffee—just regular Folgers, nothing fancy, run it through the drip machine—and I sit there at the kitchen table in the dark before my wife wakes up, just drinking my coffee and going over the route sheet for the day in my head. I'm planning out which crew is taking the big trailer with the two zero-turns, and who's taking the smaller setup for the residential stuff over in the historic district where the driveways are too narrow for the big rigs. I'm still engaged in all of that. \n\nIf I thought life wasn't worth living, I wouldn't care about any of that, right? I wouldn't care if the blades on the mowers were sharpened or if the two-cycle oil was mixed right for the trimmers. But I do care. I mean, I have responsibilities. I have a wife who relies on me, I have a mortgage on a three-bedroom ranch house that we bought fifteen years ago, and I have guys on my crew who need me to keep the business running so they can feed their own families. Like my foreman, Dave, he just had his second kid, a little girl. If I just gave up, what happens to Dave? What happens to his family? \n\nSo, no, I don't want to die or hurt myself. I'm not looking for an exit. It's just... it's just this enduring slog. It's like I'm walking through waist-deep mud every single day. I'm still walking, I'm still putting one foot in front of the other, and I'm still getting to where I need to go. It just takes so much effort to drag myself through it, and there's no joy in the journey anymore. I'm just doing what needs to be done because I'm a responsible guy and that's what you do. You work hard, you pay your bills, you take care of your people. But as far as actually wanting to end it all? No, absolutely not. I've never had a thought like that cross my mind.\n\nDoctor: Even though you still do the things you enjoy, like fishing or helping your daughter with her project, do you find that you don’t feel any pleasure or satisfaction while doing them?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I still get a kick out of the things I really like doing. It's not like I'm completely numb or anything. Like with the fishing... if I take my old fourteen-foot aluminum jon boat out to the reservoir—usually I try to get out there by 5:30 in the morning on a Sunday, right when the mist is still coming off the water, you know? I'll take my favorite rod, it's just a medium-heavy action with a baitcaster reel, and I'll tie on a chartreuse spinnerbait. And I'll tell you, when I'm casting out near the lily pads and I feel that sudden jerk on the line, that heavy thump when a good-sized largemouth bass hits the lure... yeah, I definitely still feel that rush. I get a lot of satisfaction out of reeling it in, netting it, just admiring the fish before I toss it back. I smile. I enjoy it. I really do. \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing with my daughter. She's twenty-two now, just rented this little duplex over on the east side, and she wanted to put in some raised garden beds for tomatoes and peppers. Well, being in landscaping, that's right up my alley, so I went over there last weekend to help her. We drove my truck down to the lumber yard and picked out some nice, thick cedar planks—you always want to use cedar or redwood for vegetables, never the pressure-treated stuff because the chemicals can leach into the soil, and I explained all that to her. We spent the whole afternoon measuring, cutting the boards, driving in these three-inch star-bit deck screws so they won't strip out... and when we finally got them all boxed up and perfectly leveled out in her yard, and I saw how proud she was? I felt great. I felt real pleasure in that. We sat on the tailgate and drank a couple of Cokes and just admired the work, and I felt like a good dad. \n\nSo, no, when I'm actually doing those things, the fishing, the projects with her, I'm definitely feeling the satisfaction. It's just that... well, the fishing trip ends, or the garden beds are built, and I have to go back to the regular day-to-day, and that's when that heavy, gray slog I was telling you about just settles right back over my shoulders. But the actual activities themselves? No, I haven't lost the joy in those. I still feel it just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that, outside of those specific activities like fishing or working with your daughter, most things in life don’t give you much pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say that either. It's not just the fishing or the big projects that I like. I still enjoy plenty of regular, everyday things, too. \n\nI mean, take Friday nights, for example. My wife and I, we have this little tradition where I'll stop by Miller's Butcher Shop on the way home from the yard. It's this family-owned place off Route 9—they've been there since I was a kid, actually, I went to high school with the owner's younger brother, Mike. Anyway, I'll go in and pick up two nice, thick ribeyes. Bone-in, usually, because the flavor is just better. I take them home, rub them down with just some coarse kosher salt, cracked black pepper, and maybe a little garlic powder, and then I throw them on my Weber kettle grill. I've always preferred charcoal over gas. I use those Kingsford briquettes, and I always get them going in a metal chimney starter so you don't get that nasty lighter fluid taste on the meat. When I'm standing out there on the back patio, smelling that smoke, holding a cold beer, and then we finally sit down at the table and that steak is cooked a perfect medium-rare... I enjoy the heck out of that. That gives me a lot of pleasure. A really good meal still tastes just as good as it always did. \n\nAnd it's the same thing with other stuff, too. Like if I'm watching a football game on a Sunday afternoon. I've been a die-hard fan of my team for thirty years, through all the terrible seasons and the good ones. If they pull off a big win in the fourth quarter, or the quarterback throws a perfect fifty-yard bomb down the sideline, I'm out of my seat, cheering, high-fiving my wife, just feeling really good about it. Or even just listening to classic rock on the radio while I'm driving the work truck between job sites—if a good Bob Seger song comes on, I'll turn it up and sing along, and I genuinely enjoy it. \n\nSo it's not like everything else is just dead to me, or like everything tastes like cardboard. I still like a good joke, I still like a good meal, I still like my sports. It's just... like I was trying to explain before, it's the background noise. It's what happens when the steak is eaten, or the game goes off, or the song ends, and I'm just left sitting there with my own thoughts. That's when I feel that heavy, gray slog I was talking about. But the actual things in life? The things that are supposed to be fun or taste good? No, I still get plenty of enjoyment out of them. I'm not walking around completely numb to the good stuff. I just wanted to make sure I'm explaining that right, because I know it sounds a little contradictory. You'd think if a guy feels like he's dragging a hundred-pound weight around all day, he wouldn't care about a ribeye steak or a football game, but I really do. I still enjoy all that stuff just fine.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when even the things you used to enjoy, like your steak on Friday nights or watching football, didn’t feel as good as they used to—even if just for a day or two?\n\nPatient: Well, I've been trying to think if there was ever a time like that, but honestly... no, I really haven't noticed anything like that. Not even for a day. I mean, it’s a fair question, but if I’m eating that ribeye, I’m enjoying it. Every single time. \n\nTake last November, for instance. Right around Thanksgiving, we had this massive, unexpected cold snap. The ground froze solid way earlier than usual, which is an absolute nightmare in my line of work because we were still trying to finish up a big hardscaping job over at that new subdivision on Elm Street. Have you seen those new houses? They're huge, but the lots are tiny. Anyway, laying patio pavers when the ground is freezing up is just... well, it's a massive headache. You have to bring in these expensive ground heaters, the polymeric sand doesn't settle right between the joints, the guys on the crew are miserable... I was stressed out of my mind, working fourteen-hour days, coming home just completely wiped out, bone-tired, and that heavy, gray slog feeling I told you about was just crushing me worse than ever. \n\nBut even then, even on the absolute worst day of that week—it was a Thursday, I remember specifically because my work truck's heater core decided to blow out on the drive home, so I was freezing my tail off in the cab—when I finally walked through the front door and my wife had made a big pot of her homemade chili... man, that chili tasted amazing. She uses the ground chuck from Miller's, the same place I get the steaks, and she puts in these dark red kidney beans and just the right amount of cumin and chili powder. I sat down at the kitchen table, ate two massive bowls of it, put on the Thursday night football game, and for those couple of hours, I was completely locked in. I loved every single bite, and I was yelling at the TV when the refs blew a totally obvious holding call in the third quarter. \n\nSo, even on my absolute worst days, when that heavy feeling is just pushing me down into the dirt, the good stuff still feels good. The food still tastes great, the games are still exciting, the fishing is still fun. It never really dips or goes away, not even for a day or two. It's just that the minute the game is over and the TV goes off, or the bowl is empty, I'm right back in the mud. But the enjoyment itself? No, I've never lost that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. We drove my truck down to the lumber yard and picked out some nice, thick cedar planks—you always want to use cedar or redwood for vegetables, never the pressure-treated stuff because the chemicals can leach into the soil, and I explained all that to her. We spent the whole afternoon measuring, cutting the boards, driving in these three-inch star-bit deck screws so they won't strip out... and when we finally got them all boxed up and perfectly leveled out in her yard, and I saw how proud she was? I felt great. I felt real pleasure in that. We sat on the tailgate and drank a couple of Cokes and just admired the work, and I felt like a good dad. \n\nSo, no, when I'm actually doing those things, the fishing, the projects with her, I'm definitely feeling the satisfaction. It's just that... well, the fishing trip ends, or the garden beds are built, and I have to go back to the regular day-to-day, and that's when that heavy, gray slog I was telling you about just settles right back over my shoulders. But the actual activities themselves? No, I haven't lost the joy in those. I still feel it just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that, outside of those specific activities like fishing or working with your daughter, most things in life don’t give you much pleasure anymore?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say that either. It's not just the fishing or the big projects that I like. I still enjoy plenty of regular, everyday things, too. \n\nI mean, take Friday nights, for example. My wife and I, we have this little tradition where I'll stop by Miller's Butcher Shop on the way home from the yard. It's this family-owned place off Route 9—they've been there since I was a kid, actually, I went to high school with the owner's younger brother, Mike. Anyway, I'll go in and pick up two nice, thick ribeyes. Bone-in, usually, because the flavor is just better. I take them home, rub them down with just some coarse kosher salt, cracked black pepper, and maybe a little garlic powder, and then I throw them on my Weber kettle grill. I've always preferred charcoal over gas. I use those Kingsford briquettes, and I always get them going in a metal chimney starter so you don't get that nasty lighter fluid taste on the meat. When I'm standing out there on the back patio, smelling that smoke, holding a cold beer, and then we finally sit down at the table and that steak is cooked a perfect medium-rare... I enjoy the heck out of that. That gives me a lot of pleasure. A really good meal still tastes just as good as it always did. \n\nAnd it's the same thing with other stuff, too. Like if I'm watching a football game on a Sunday afternoon. I've been a die-hard fan of my team for thirty years, through all the terrible seasons and the good ones. If they pull off a big win in the fourth quarter, or the quarterback throws a perfect fifty-yard bomb down the sideline, I'm out of my seat, cheering, high-fiving my wife, just feeling really good about it. Or even just listening to classic rock on the radio while I'm driving the work truck between job sites—if a good Bob Seger song comes on, I'll turn it up and sing along, and I genuinely enjoy it. \n\nSo it's not like everything else is just dead to me, or like everything tastes like cardboard. I still like a good joke, I still like a good meal, I still like my sports. It's just... like I was trying to explain before, it's the background noise. It's what happens when the steak is eaten, or the game goes off, or the song ends, and I'm just left sitting there with my own thoughts. That's when I feel that heavy, gray slog I was talking about. But the actual things in life? The things that are supposed to be fun or taste good? No, I still get plenty of enjoyment out of them. I'm not walking around completely numb to the good stuff. I just wanted to make sure I'm explaining that right, because I know it sounds a little contradictory. You'd think if a guy feels like he's dragging a hundred-pound weight around all day, he wouldn't care about a ribeye steak or a football game, but I really do. I still enjoy all that stuff just fine.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when even the things you used to enjoy, like your steak on Friday nights or watching football, didn’t feel as good as they used to—even if just for a day or two?\n\nPatient: Well, I've been trying to think if there was ever a time like that, but honestly... no, I really haven't noticed anything like that. Not even for a day. I mean, it’s a fair question, but if I’m eating that ribeye, I’m enjoying it. Every single time. \n\nTake last November, for instance. Right around Thanksgiving, we had this massive, unexpected cold snap. The ground froze solid way earlier than usual, which is an absolute nightmare in my line of work because we were still trying to finish up a big hardscaping job over at that new subdivision on Elm Street. Have you seen those new houses? They're huge, but the lots are tiny. Anyway, laying patio pavers when the ground is freezing up is just... well, it's a massive headache. You have to bring in these expensive ground heaters, the polymeric sand doesn't settle right between the joints, the guys on the crew are miserable... I was stressed out of my mind, working fourteen-hour days, coming home just completely wiped out, bone-tired, and that heavy, gray slog feeling I told you about was just crushing me worse than ever. \n\nBut even then, even on the absolute worst day of that week—it was a Thursday, I remember specifically because my work truck's heater core decided to blow out on the drive home, so I was freezing my tail off in the cab—when I finally walked through the front door and my wife had made a big pot of her homemade chili... man, that chili tasted amazing. She uses the ground chuck from Miller's, the same place I get the steaks, and she puts in these dark red kidney beans and just the right amount of cumin and chili powder. I sat down at the kitchen table, ate two massive bowls of it, put on the Thursday night football game, and for those couple of hours, I was completely locked in. I loved every single bite, and I was yelling at the TV when the refs blew a totally obvious holding call in the third quarter. \n\nSo, even on my absolute worst days, when that heavy feeling is just pushing me down into the dirt, the good stuff still feels good. The food still tastes great, the games are still exciting, the fishing is still fun. It never really dips or goes away, not even for a day or two. It's just that the minute the game is over and the TV goes off, or the bowl is empty, I'm right back in the mud. But the enjoyment itself? No, I've never lost that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, two years... yeah, definitely two years. Actually, it's been longer than that. If I really sit down and trace it back, it’s been going on for about three years and three months now. 

I know the exact timeframe because it started right around the time our big groundskeeping contract changed at work. I mainly handle these large commercial properties—corporate parks, mostly, over in the west end of the county—and the property management company decided to completely renegotiate the terms of our service agreement. They brought in all these new stipulations, you know, demanding weekly mulching checks, constant seasonal flower rotations, power washing the concrete walkways... things we used to do on a monthly rotating basis, suddenly they wanted it all done constantly. 

So our schedule just got completely upended. The longer days just completely took over my life. I went from having a normal, predictable routine to leaving the house before the sun even came up and not getting my work boots off until well after dark. You're just working and working, running the zero-turn mowers, hauling brush, dealing with the property managers complaining about every little weed, and you have absolutely no time for yourself during the week. That’s exactly when that heavy, gray slog I was telling you about just sort of draped itself over my shoulders. 

I remember thinking at first that maybe I was just worn out from the new hours, you know? Just an adjustment period.
